New Democratic Congress will likely strip the gilding from Trump's Golden Dome

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“There’s no doubt that whatever it’s going to be called, it won’t be called Golden Dome.”

Two of the top lawmakers backing the Pentagon’s Golden Dome missile defense shield are among the wave of Republicans leaving Congress in the coming Democratic takeover, raising questions about the future of President Donald Trump’s signature second-term defense initiative.

The shakeup on Capitol Hill comes after military officials signaled in recent months that they might backpedal from one element Trump asked for in Golden Dome: the capability to shoot down missiles in their initial boost phase shortly after launch.

The Golden Dome has escaped serious congressional scrutiny since Trump announced the program in January 2025, one week into his second term in the White House. That lack of scrutiny will change as Democrats take responsibility for writing the Pentagon’s budget, introduce limits on funding, and put forth bills to strip out Golden Dome’s most controversial component and give the program a new name.

That’s the opinion of a half-dozen current and former officials in industry and government. Ars spoke with sources before and after last week’s congressional elections to get a sense of how a new Democratic Congress might make its imprint on missile defense.

It’s important to know that much of what the Trump administration calls “Golden Dome” was already in development (or in some cases, operational) when Trump signed the executive order outlining the initiative 22 months ago. Trump’s order created a new program to consolidate the Pentagon’s existing missile defense efforts, accelerate work already underway on new Ground-Based Interceptors (GBIs), and deploy a new layer of orbiting sensors and Space-Based Interceptors (SBIs) to defend the US homeland against both traditional ballistic missiles and new emerging threats like drones and hypersonic weapons.

The Golden Dome was immediately politicized. The first tranche of its funding—approximately $24 billion—was part of Trump’s so-called “One Big Beautiful Bill,” along with some of the White House’s other contentious policy priorities. The bill passed Congress on a party-line vote. This meant the budget for Golden Dome was not part of the normal military budgeting process in Congress, which usually involves both parties. Instead, it passed Congress through a controversial shortcut known as reconciliation.

Sen. Mike Rounds (R-S.D.) said in a September hearing that the White House’s decision to link Golden Dome’s first tranche of funding to a partisan reconciliation bill was unnecessary. “This should have been on a bipartisan basis,” Rounds said. Indeed, Capitol Hill Republicans refused the Trump administration’s effort to add $350 billion to the Pentagon’s budget this year through a similar reconciliation mechanism.

Lawmakers also formed an all-Republican “Golden Dome Caucus” soon after the unveiling of Golden Dome. Seven House members of the caucus either lost their reelection bids or did not run for reelection at all in the 2026 midterms. These include the caucus’s co-chair, Rep. Jeff Crank (R-Colo.), and the chair of the House subcommittee overseeing Golden Dome, Rep. Scott DesJarlais (R-Tenn.). Both are among the Golden Dome’s biggest cheerleaders in Congress.

The Democratic ranking member of the House Subcommittee on Strategic Forces, Rep. Scott Moulton (D-Mass.), is retiring from Congress. That leaves Rep. John Garamendi (D-Calif.) as the next-senior Democrat on the subcommittee, with a path to becoming chairman when the new Congress convenes in January. In remarks earlier this year, he called Golden Dome a “massive, extraordinarily expensive, and ultimately inadequate defensive system.”

“When you use the words Golden Dome, now it’s political, and when you politicize national security, you get a political response, so it is damaging,” said John Plumb, who supervised military space policy for the Biden administration, in a panel discussion at the CyberSat conference this week.

The Trump administration projects Golden Dome will cost $185 billion over the next 10 years, a figure that’s hard to verify with the scarce information military officials have released about how the system will work.

Other cost estimates are significantly higher. The Congressional Budget Office concluded that a national missile defense system meeting the requirements set out in Trump’s executive order would cost about $1.2 trillion. The American Enterprise Institute released a report last year with a menu of potential Golden Dome architectures, ranging from a quarter-trillion dollars (without SBIs) to $3.6 trillion at the high end.

Pentagon officials chafe at these numbers, claiming they do not take into account the military’s assumptions for low-cost launches and mass-produced satellite networks.

The military envisions at least hundreds, and perhaps thousands, of Space-Based Interceptors in the uppermost layer of Golden Dome. These interceptors would loiter in orbit to wait for a missile attack. SBIs have never been placed into orbit before, despite decades of research and development. The idea has been around at least since the 1980s, when former President Ronald Reagan pushed the Strategic Defense Initiative, or “Star Wars,” weapons program.

“The most ambitious element of Golden Dome is the Space-Based Interceptor layer, and that is going to face significant technical challenges,” said Sen. Mark Kelly (D-Ariz.), in a congressional hearing earlier this year.

“The cost, the physics, the likely reliability, in my view, do not support a full-scale space-based intercept architecture,” said Kelly, a former Navy combat pilot and astronaut. “This means that we could spend more than a trillion dollars and end up with a system that’s not going to work, and it’s not going to meaningfully improve our security.”

Some critics call out Golden Dome’s potential negative impact on global stability, fearing it could spark an arms race driving the world’s other nuclear powers to expand their arsenals. “It motivates the other side to just build more missiles because the easiest way to overcome a defense like that is just to buy more,” said Frank Kendall, who served as secretary of the Air Force in the Biden administration.

Others point to the risks of weaponizing space—a similar argument used against the Reagan-era Star Wars program. Secretary of the Air Force Troy Meink lifted that taboo in September with the first public disclosure that the US Space Force already has weapons in orbit.

But there’s more to the story than SBIs. The satellites and radars that detect and track missile launches are either in use today or were already in development during previous administrations. The same goes for Ground-Based Interceptors, which have been deployed for decades to take out missiles as they approach their targets. Under President Biden, the Pentagon’s Missile Defense Agency awarded Lockheed Martin a $17 billion contract to develop a next-generation Ground-Based Interceptor to defend against more sophisticated missile threats.

The $24 billion Congress approved for Golden Dome last year was a one-time allocation for fiscal year 2026, which ended on October 1. The program’s funding dried up last month because the government is currently operating under a continuing resolution—an extension of last year’s regular military budget, which, of course, did not include the $24 billion Golden Dome infusion routed through reconciliation. The preliminary funding bills in the House and Senate for fiscal year 2027, if passed as-is, would provide only a fraction of the more than $17 billion the Trump administration requested for Golden Dome in this budget cycle.

That’s because the Trump administration again separated a significant part of its record $1.5 trillion topline military budget request into a reconciliation package outside of the normal congressional appropriations process. The supplemental $350 billion funding request—which included funds for Golden Dome—went nowhere in the Republican-controlled Congress. And it won’t happen when Democrats take the gavels next year, either.

It’s impossible to judge the realism of the Pentagon’s aim to get a first draft of Golden Dome up and running by the end 0f 2028, before the end of Trump’s presidency, because so much of the system is classified—but skepticism is the safer bet. Military officials have said little about Golden Dome’s design, architecture, or concept of operations, citing a national security imperative for secrecy.

One thing we do know is that a homeland defense shield like Golden Dome will need international partners to host radar sensors, ground stations, and missile batteries. Canada is an obvious target for such a partnership, and a fully deployed Golden Dome would notionally provide protection over all of North America.

However, this part of the program is off to a slow start. Michael Guetlein, the four-star Space Force general in charge of Golden Dome development, said earlier this year that tariffs—a core Trump administration policy—were limiting his interactions with foreign governments.

So what can we expect when the next Congress convenes with Democratic majorities in January? The consensus among the experts Ars interviewed is that military leaders will still push for new options in missile defense, a need they say is underscored by their experience during the US war with Iran and Russia’s persistent missile strikes on Ukraine.

Modern missiles and drones can easily dodge existing defense systems. The stark expense of existing Western interceptors compared to the cost of Iranian missiles and drones can make defenders think twice about whether to shoot or keep their powder dry for a future attack.

“I feel like the entire effort would benefit from a better problem statement,” one former military space official told Ars. “Why do we think we need something that we don’t currently have? What’s changed? What’s different? What is it?”

This official offered some answers to these questions: “We are not in the 1970s or 1980s anymore, where it was us and the Soviet Union. The vast share of strategic threats to us were strategic ballistic missiles that we could see coming. That’s how things used to be.”

“Today’s environment is different,” he continued. “You have a host of strategic threats that are making classic ballistic missiles shrink in share. You’ve got hypersonic weapons. You’ve got endo-atmospheric hypersonic vehicles. You have increasingly accurate and harder to detect cruise missiles. It’s no longer just Russia. It’s China now, and to a lesser extent, North Korea and Iran.”

These new missile threats and the ubiquity of one-way attack drones make US and allied bases more susceptible to attack, the official said.

“What do we do about it? Do we just build more Mutually Assured Destruction platforms, or is there something else we can do? I think that’s what gets you to maybe we should look at ways to at least interfere with a potential attack against us.”

But some parts of Golden Dome, if followed to the letter of Trump’s executive order, simply may not work, at least at the scale required for global coverage, the official told Ars. One of them is a requirement to develop SBIs that can destroy enemy missiles during their boost phase soon after launch. This is in contrast to midcourse intercept in space or terminal intercept as the missile homes in on its target.

“The boost-phase intercept is a much bigger challenge,” the former military official said. “I’m probably going to have to have an interceptor that can survive reentry, track through reentry, and hit something while it’s still in the atmosphere. Hitting something in midcourse, as it’s left the atmosphere and is in space is just an easier problem… The original executive order talked about boost-phase intercept, and I was always skeptical about that one.”

An SBI aimed at a boost-phase ballistic missile would have to look down toward the Earth, not up toward space. The interceptor would have to distinguish the missile it’s tracking against the background of Earth, with clouds, cities, land, and water. It’s much easier to do that against the cold blackness of space. There are some benefits to a boost-phase intercept. For example, the ballistic missile is traveling slower soon after launch, and you might have another shot to intercept it if you miss.

The window for intercepting a missile in boost phase is also exceptionally narrow. The time from a launch detection to a shoot-down decision might be less than a minute, and a commander charged with making a “kill decision” would likely want more time to understand what’s happening before pulling the trigger on an interceptor.

“When does the person who has the authority for weapons engagement release the weapon? If your answer is it’s all automated, that’s interesting, but it’s also a little scary,” the former military official said. “Now I’m going to explain to the president why you just killed three taikonauts (Chinese astronauts) coming off a launch pad because no human was there. If you do need a human to say, ‘OK, I authorize release of the weapon,’ then that’s a time gap.”

Guetlein, the man in charge of Golden Dome, has acknowledged this challenge.

“I do believe that the technology exists to get after that threat today, and we believe we can solve it, but… can I do it at scale, and can I do it affordably? That’s going to be the huge challenge for boost-phase intercept,” Guetlein told lawmakers in an April hearing. “I will tell you, because we are so focused on affordability, if we cannot do it affordably, we will not go into production.”

There are other ways to take down missiles if engineers can’t close the case for boost-phase intercept, Guetlein said. “If boost-phase intercept from space is not affordable and [doesn’t] scale well, we will not produce it because we have other options to get after it.”

If proven feasible, directed-energy weapons using microwave or laser beams would provide US forces near-endless “magazine depth” in responding to massive salvos of missiles and drones, eschewing the need for “kinetic” SBIs for missile defense. Sen. Angus King (I-Maine) said in a hearing earlier this year that the Space Force should spend more on directed-energy research than on space-based kinetic kill vehicles.

Plumb, the former Biden military space policy chief, pointed to bipartisan support for the Pentagon’s existing missile defense programs, along with new initiatives to improve tracking hypersonic missiles. “I think any administration, Republican or Democrat, is going to continue to improve missile defenses for the US, 100 percent,” he said, adding that it is “probably unlikely” that SBIs survive the coming cull.

So what do the people actually building Golden Dome think? Their answer is similar. Some missile defense priorities will survive, but maybe with a new name.

The name Golden Dome is a “brand,” said Dylan Taylor, chairman and CEO of Voyager Technologies, which works with Anduril on SBIs. “It’s a proxy for layers of a protective onion, if you will, and there are elements of what is being called Golden Dome, where the technology is very advanced, and it works. The systems work. They’re more functional than I think people have reported on or have indicated.”

“Golden Dome is a lot more than SBIs,” said another executive at a space company working on the project. “It’s one layer and element. We believe that there’s bipartisan support for this capability because it is a required capability for national security. The question is whether it can be achieved at a price that makes it executable and palatable. The technical aspect of this closes, and there’s just a question about scalability and cost.”

The executive told Ars that newer, smaller space companies can do the job for less money than the Pentagon’s legacy contractors. Many companies are spending private capital on Golden Dome-related research and development with the promise of future Pentagon business. Military leaders have added stops on Wall Street and with venture capital firms to their usual tours of bases, command centers, and test ranges.

“There’s real hardware being built by all the players,” the official said, suggesting optimism about fielding a working preliminary version of Golden Dome by 2028. “From us, on our execution to date, we’ve conducted engine tests and thermal tests and communications tests.

“My fiduciary responsibility is to turn our investment into these critical technologies into future value, and I wouldn’t have made that call had I not been comfortable with the survivability of the program, assuming we can achieve the technical and financial targets, which we’ve convinced ourselves we can,” the official said.

Rocket Lab is another company with business ties to Golden Dome. The company’s founder and CEO, Peter Beck, told Ars he was convinced missile defense will remain a priority no matter who controls Congress or the White House.

“Is it a national security imperative that we go to the Moon? Probably not. Is it a national security imperative that we can protect the nation from incoming missile threats? Yes,” Beck said. “With these sorts of things, I’d say it’s what shape and scale they are, not whether they survive.”

“There are elements of Golden Dome that I don’t think are going to be politically vulnerable, and there will be other parts of Golden Dome that, of course, will be,” Taylor said. “The point I want to emphasize is people who say, ‘Well, Golden Dome is dead; the whole thing is dead,’ they don’t necessarily understand that there are four or five or six layers to the system, and some are vulnerable and some are not.”

Voyager’s expertise for missile defense is in advanced propulsion, and Taylor said those technologies are relevant for a range of systems, not just Golden Dome.

“There’s no doubt that whatever it’s going to be called, it won’t be called Golden Dome, and there will definitely be machinations in the system, but it won’t be a ‘throw the baby out with the bath water’ approach,” Taylor said. “Given the risk profile, I don’t see that happening.”

In a Senate hearing in September, Tom Karako, director of the Missile Defense Project at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, proposed the idea of a name change as one option to “depoliticize” the missile defense issue.

“It is no secret that the ‘Golden Dome’ moniker has contributed to political polarization around the effort,” Karako said in his testimony. “Several options exist to depolarize and depoliticize the effort. It belongs to Congress to name or rename executive departments—witness the pending proposal to rename the Department of Defense as the Department of War. Congress likewise has legislative power to rename the Golden Dome initiative in a future national defense authorization act.”

“There are things in the program that make sense,” said Sen. Kelly, who serves on the Senate Subcommittee for Strategic Forces. These include new capabilities for “detecting launches,” he said. “The Space-Based Interceptor part is really, really hard, and the physics clearly favors the offense,” Kelly said. “We could go down this road, spend hundreds of billions of dollars, and not get any return on that investment, and missile defense is too important to pursue this just based on aspiration alone.”

Kathy Warden, the CEO of Northrop Grumman, acknowledged last year that with SBIs, “more invention is required than other parts of the [Golden Dome] architecture.”

The most recent trends in warfare, as seen in the Middle East and Ukraine, highlight the need for cheaper, mass-produced countermeasures to use against massive drone and missile attacks. In other words, it requires magazine depth, as some military commanders like to say. Democrats seem likely to conclude that the path to magazine depth does not include SBIs.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Nvidia's CEO turmoil gives newly empowered Democrats fresh ammunition in pursuit of oversight

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's close ties to the U.S. government were already poised to loosen with Democrats winning control of Congress and promising far more oversight of AI development than what the chip giant has experienced under Republican leadership.

That was before Huang admitted on Sunday to having an extramarital affair with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy. The woman, Alice Huang, is affiliated with an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed that's been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia's artificial intelligence chips.

Huang apologized to staffers in a note on Sunday for being "unfaithful to my wife," and said the company's board was investigating any related national security concerns. Nvidia's board said it has "confidence in Jensen," who co-founded the company in 1993 and has led it ever since, building a net worth of more than $150 billion in the process.

Even before last week's midterm elections, which saw Democrats win sweeping majorities in the House and Senate, vocal party leaders wanted answers from Huang about China's access to advanced Nvidia chips and his company's lobbying for favorable legislation. Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts said days before the election, when the spy story was first surfacing, that "we must pass bipartisan legislation to keep our advanced AI technology out of China's hands." Months before that, in June, Huang declined an invitation from Warren to testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

With Democrats about to take the gavel, Delaware Sen. Chris Coons, who is in line to chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, wrote on X on Saturday that "Congress will investigate and get answers," following the allegations that appeared in a New York Times report Friday.

It's a sharp reversal for Huang, who gained first-name status with President Donald Trump and has been closely allied with the administration in trying to limit AI regulation. Nvidia shares plunged 12% in the two days after the election, pacing a drop in AI-related stocks, on fears that incoming lawmakers, at the federal and state levels, and a host of newly passed ballot measures will slow the buildout and halt the AI boom.

Huang is a singular voice in the AI industry, as his company's technology is used to train nearly all major AI models. He's also turned Nvidia into an essential source of financing for AI startups, and pulled together some of the biggest names on Wall Street into a group that announced plans in August to raise $500 billion for investing in the buildout.

At Nvidia, Huang is the sole public face of the company and has no clear second-in-command. He decides which projects are a strategic priority, and frequently sends short emails to individual engineers about details. Huang has said he has 60 direct reports and reviews every employee's salary. Meanwhile, he packs his calendar with meetings with world leaders and CEOs, alongside keynote speeches at conferences and trade shows.

"Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he IS Nvidia," Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon wrote in a note on Sunday, adding that it's not clear who would succeed him.

Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, said in an email that "Jensen holding so much power is why Nvidia moves faster than anyone."

Unlike many of his tech peers, Huang is not chairman of the board. Rather, he's one of 11 board members, and there's no designated chair.

One investor close to Huang told CNBC that, depending on what the board's investigation finds, he could foresee Huang taking a leave of absence.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia," a company spokesperson said in a statement. "Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption."

But scrutiny of Nvidia is set to intensify on Wall Street and in Washington, where Huang's level of influence is almost certain to change.

He has been a major advocate for more chip exports to China, arguing that it's better for AI labs there to build on American technology than to encourage the development of domestic alternatives. Huang joined Trump on Air Force One in May on a China trip that's now raised additional questions about what the administration and Huang knew about Chinese spying at the time.

One question Democrats are likely to probe is whether the Trump administration was aware of Huang's relationship with the alleged Chinese spy, and whether the Nvidia CEO influenced American policy, said Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at the Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

"That is going to be very high up on the Democrats' lists of people to investigate to try to figure out to what extent was the Trump administration aware of what was going on," Bresnick said.

But pressure isn't just coming from Democrats. House Republican leaders have already asked Huang for a closed-door briefing on Chinese spying issues later this month.

Trump has continued to back Huang, telling reporters on Sunday that he's an "honorable guy" and has "done an amazing job for our country."

"I think he probably has an excuse for it," Trump said of the incident.

Amid all the noise about the election results and the spying revelations, Nvidia is facing a crucial stretch on the markets.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Nebius, which are both backed by Nvidia and are big buyers of its chips, are scheduled to report earnings this week after their stocks lost roughly half their value over the past month. Investors are particularly concerned about their hefty debt loads and how they'll continue to fund their buildouts with interest rates rising and lenders turning skittish.

Nvidia has its own earnings report coming on Nov. 17, when Huang, assuming he sticks with the typical format, will face questions from analysts. And then there's the Anthropic initial public offering, which is critical for Nvidia as it's both an investor in the Claude creator and counts the AI lab as a significant customer.

A couple of weeks after earnings, Nvidia is hosting a large conference in Washington, D.C., where Huang is scheduled to give the keynote on Dec. 1.

Bullish investors and analysts can still point to the insatiable demand for Nvidia's technology. The company recently projected 70% sales growth in its upcoming fiscal year. Analysts polled by FactSet expect nearly $694 billion in fiscal 2028 sales.

"We believe demand for the company's products seems unlikely to be affected," Rasgon wrote. "But it seems plausible to postulate increased risks (regulatory etc.) as AI buildouts become an increasingly political issue."

— CNBC's Seema Mody, Katie Tarasov, Ashley Capoot and MacKenzie Sigalos contributed reporting to this report.

Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

New Hulu Doc ‘Get Huge’ Shows the Deadly Toll of Looksmaxxing

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Before Clavicular, before Androgenic, before the Tate brothers, before bonesmashing and frame-mogging and the manosphere and looksmaxxing entered the online lexicon, there was Tony Huge.

A 44-year-old former model turned lawyer turned bodybuilder turned supplement impresario, Huge fled the United States for Thailand to escape legal pressure on his company from the Food and Drug Administration and to build out a “harem” of young, nubile Thai women. With his bulky frame, his sex-and-drugs-fueled lifestyle, and his motto “a day natty [natural] is a day wasted,” Huge became an icon for what was then the fledgling biohacking community. He amassed a huge following of disaffected, lonely young men, some of whom moved to Thailand to live with him. Then, some of them started dying.

Huge is one of the main focuses of Get Huge: Looksmaxxing to Death, a bone-chilling documentary series hitting Hulu on November 10. Directed by Stacey Lee and executive-produced by the journalist Jennings Brown, Get Huge kicks off with the untimely death of “Leo and Longevity,” aka Laith Abdallah Algaz, a biohacker and penis enlargement influencer who was found deceased in Huge’s home in January 2023.

What follows, however, is not a typical true-crime documentary. It’s a dizzying investigation into the hedonistic, self-destructive, and often deadly world of self-optimization, in which dudes inject themselves with pig’s brains and liquified gold, alliances shift at the drop of a hat, and you’re only as good as your last high-performing Instagram Reel. (A warning, spoilers for Get Huge follow.)

Since 2023, Huge has opened up his Pattaya, Thailand, home to the Get Huge team, offering “full transparency” into what Lee refers to as “this 24/7 performance of being a man.”

“It was, ‘Here’s my harem, here’s my other men that I’m leading, here’s my muscles, here’s my drugs,’” she says. But as her crew got further and further into the filmmaking process, it became clear that what was going on behind the scenes—not just the turmoil within Huge’s inner circle but also the hangers-on dropping like flies around him—was far more interesting.

“Therein lies the problem for the young kid watching at home who is watching this as a blueprint for their life. They can't discern what's real and what's not,” Lee says. “Even I was having trouble discerning what was real and what’s not.”

In some respects, Get Huge is about the rise of looksmaxxing, the wildly popular and dangerous online self-optimization movement popularized by manosphere influencers such as Clavicular and Androgenic. (The filmmakers say Clavicular participated in initial talks with the producers to participate in the documentary but ultimately declined; Androgenic plays a small but key role in the series.)

Tony Huge in the new docuseries, Get Huge.

But T. Jennings Brown, a journalist and podcast producer, started delving into Huge’s world in 2023, long before Clav became a household name. Brown and his producing partner, Brett Neitchin, were obsessed with the manosphere and how the internet has ushered in extreme and rigid views of masculinity. “I went to school in Kansas and was short and felt insecure about that and always thought someday I’d save up for leg extensions,” he said. “So I’ve always had a horrified fascination with this world.”

Lee came on board as director a year later, just as the manosphere was starting to become a major talking point with the 2024 election. “It was such an absurd story—I mean, a penis influencer dying in Thailand,” she says. “But what was really interesting beyond the subject matter was the deeper story—why are men going to Thailand, and why is this kind of masculinity becoming more and more prevalent in online spaces?”

In some respects, Get Huge is a relatively straightforward true-crime story. Algaz was found deceased in Huge’s home with his pants around his ankles in January 2023, and Thai police ultimately concluded he died of blunt force head trauma. (The case is still open.) Internet sleuths in the looksmaxxing community immediately started pointing fingers at Huge, who had also been linked to two other prominent looksmaxxers, Nick Weisser and Josh Brooks, who died under equally mysterious circumstances in 2018 and 2021, respectively.

But as the filmmakers delved deeper into Huge’s world, they realized that was not the appropriate tack. “True crime became a vehicle through which to actually explore and understand this world, the stakes of it, the responsibilities of it, what keeps men there after these tragedies happen,” Lee says. “We wanted to know not only what draws them in but what keeps them there.” (That was particularly true for Algaz, who had recently abandoned his wife and child in Los Angeles before coming to live with Huge in Thailand.)

In some ways, the question of what keeps Huge’s acolytes in Thailand is the driving force of the series, particularly after the bodybuilder and influencer Connor Murphy (who one of the men in Huge’s circle refers to as “the ultimate chad”) joins the inner circle. As the men resort to increasingly extreme looksmaxxing methods, with Hughes even undergoing a psychotic break as a result of his drug use, they remain entrenched in Huge’s circle until Huge essentially decides to cast them out.

Huge did not respond to WIRED’s request for comment on the documentary.

Though the men frequently cite the importance of male friendship in their lives, they are constantly bickering and enabling each other, filming themselves engaging in increasingly self-destructive behavior to increase their viewership. “They don’t take care of each other,” one of Hughes’ former acolytes, Elon Muscular, says in Get Huge. “They don’t uplift each other. They find ways to fucking fuck each others’ lives up with bad habits.”

There’s also, it must be said, an obvious strain of queerness throughout the series, with the men regularly taking copious amounts of drugs before having group sex with each other and a bevy of Thai sex workers, who Huge calls his “harem.”

“Bodyhacking and looksmaxxing have a very homoerotic side,” says Lee. “It’s a bunch of dudes oiled up, posing with each other in little Speedos, and many of the drugs that began circulating within the community came from gay culture. [And] I think there is a lot of closeted behavior and tendencies to be explored.”

Huge uses his “harem” to burnish his macho image, tattooing his name on one and paying for another’s breast implants. In one shocking scene, he shoots some members of the harem up with drugs to increase their sexual attraction to him. Huge is clear throughout that he is paying these women for their time, raising uncomfortable ethical questions for the audience and the filmmakers alike.

“As a woman, it was incredibly challenging to witness” the way Huge treats his girlfriends, says Lee. “I think he would rationalize what he's doing is giving these women the best versions of their lives. Same with the men—he finds them at rock bottom, and he thinks he can improve them with his chemical fixes.”

One of the most shocking moments of the series, however, happened after the crew had stopped shooting in Pattaya, when Murphy was found dead in a lake in his backyard in July 2026. Murphy had been engaging in increasingly erratic behavior, with Thai media outlets reporting that two syringes and unidentified pills were found in his vehicle after he died, and according to Get Huge, he had recently started shooting up liquid gold. Authorities attributed his cause of death to accidental drowning.

To the team behind Get Huge, however, Murphy was one more casualty associated with an increasingly dangerous movement, as well as yet another loss in Huge’s inner circle. “I think we’ve always had this feeling that this extreme form of masculinity they all believe in was a huge contributing factor” to the deaths, Lee says. In one eerie scene, Huge appears to foreshadow Murphy’s death, addressing critiques that he may be enabling his drug use by callously saying, “It’s not the end of the world if something bad happens. People also learn from that.”

Perhaps more haunting than Murphy’s death after the cameras stopped rolling, however, is that looksmaxxing has only become more popular since Get Huge started filming.

Since production wrapped four weeks before Murphy’s death, Clavicular’s public profile has only grown, with the 20-year-old influencer garnering headlines for his “Camp Looksmax” workshop and walking in a major New York Fashion Week show. The Hulu team was even hit with a cease-and-desist from Clavicular after Mitchell Jackson, Clavicular’s publicist, says they attempted to get footage from the Elena Velez fashion show without his consent, leading to him withdrawing from the project.

“Hulu needs Clavicular. Clavicular doesn’t need Hulu,” Jackson tells WIRED. “That’s why they’re using his name to promote their stupid documentary.” (Brown, who was present, refutes this, saying he was not there to film and was only attending the fashion show in a personal capacity.)

Even as Huge gets older and his audience share is dwarfed by those of the younger generation of looksmaxxers—“relevancy is everything in this world, and looksmaxxing is a young man’s game,” says Lee—the movement he has spawned continues to gain traction. The deaths of Murphy and Algaz have served as little deterrent.

“No one starts out wanting to become one of these extreme characters, and I think there’s something we can all relate to about just trying to be the best version of yourself that you can be,” says Lee. “[But] when Leo died, did anybody stop? When Connor died, did anyone stop? And I think that for me, the question is, when the goalposts keep shifting and the nihilism and the looksmaxxing and the living for today becomes more and more extreme, where does it end?”

Ink Review: Danny Boyle's Tabloid Media Origin Story Will Make You Feel Sick

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Journalism and media have undergone significant changes in the 21st century. The internet has altered everything and barriers are gone. The gatekeepers who used to be there to say what was or wasn't news don't exist, at least not in the way they used to. Director Danny Boyle has examined this "Give 'em what they want" tabloid media culture in his latest movie "Ink," which will make anyone who values real journalism sick to their stomach.

Adapting the play of the same name, the movie traces the origins of the British gossip rag The Sun, focusing on a group of misfit visionaries who had an idea for a new kind of news. Rather than simply report "the news," why not give people what they want instead?

Less a biopic a about Rupert Murdoch (played here by Guy Pearce) and his family, the real-life family the Roy clan on "Succession" is based off of, it focuses instead largely on Larry Lamb, played by "Sinners" star Jack O'Connell. Lamb was the man hand-picked by Murdoch to change The Sun from a fledgling paper into something, well, different. Driven by revenge and a sense of "I'll show 'em," Lamb, at least as presented by Boyle, is one of the most significant figures who shaped the modern era. And not in a good way, it probably goes without saying. But I'll say it anyway.

The story being told in "Ink" is an ugly one with grave consequences in the real world. Danny Boyle starts out by making this seem almost fun and exciting. Larry Lamb assembling his team has an "Ocean's Eleven" quality to it, with the kinetic energy of some of the director's best movies, most notably "Trainspotting."

Slowly but surely though, it all devolves into something more sinister. Even though news and journalism should probably be seen as a public good and an essential function of any democratic society, the need to make these publications profitable changes the game. Lamb and Rupert Murdoch lean into the "business" side of the news business, and that's where things get downright icky.

The tone of "Ink" shifts from a fun outsider tale to a dark story about taking something too far in pursuit of capitalist superiority. We see Larry Lamb lose the man he was, becoming a man that's difficult to respect. While the great newsroom movies in cinema history, such as "All the President's Men" and "Spotlight," make viewers feel the power of journalism, "Ink" forces the viewer to understand that power can and often is used in far less noble ways. Boyle is offering up his view of the men responsible for that shift.

By heading back to the past, namely the late '60s in the U.K., Boyle shines a light on the modern era. Podcasts. Blogs. Tabloids. Cable news. It all traces its roots back to this moment in history and it's hard not to leave this movie feeling some kind of way about our current media culture.

"Ink" also makes the viewer think about how accurate movies like "Spotlight" and other "based on a true story" movies are. Boyle and writer James Graham probably weren't too interested in getting everything exactly right, as this movie is about how a narrative can be shaped. Boyle and Graham shaped a narrative using real people who had real influence on the world as we know it. Accuracy isn't the point. It's about painting a picture.

"Ink" is a true showcase for Jack O'Connell. He's chewed the screen rather delightfully in movies like "28 Years Later: The Bone Temple" in recent years. But this film allows him to be more human and take the driver's seat in a grounded movie with a killer ensemble. He's the best part of a movie that also includes an excellent Claire Foy and a good-as-ever Guy Pearce.

Pearce, in particular, deserves credit for playing Rupert Murdoch with nuance. It would be easy to turn the guy who helped create Fox News into a cigar-chomping, cartoonish villain. Pearce didn't take the easy way out.

Will "Ink" rank amongst Danny Boyle's best films? Maybe not. It's tonally uneven and about as subtle as a jackhammer. But it's also got one hell of a point to make. News is kind of like vegetables: Not the most fun, but nutritious and necessary. "Ink" shines an uncomfortable light on how we, as a society, allowed news to turn from vegetables into junk food. Eat enough junk food, you'll get sick.

"Ink" hits select theatres December 11, 2026, before releasing on Netflix on January 8, 2027.

Three Bridges show spirit to earn point against Brentwood

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Bridges were desperate to get points on the board, but perhaps even more importantly, to rediscover the battling spirit that had been missing during last week's disappointing performance away at Maldon & Tiptree.

Jamie Crellin's men responded positively, showing plenty of fight and determination.

After an even opening, the hosts took the lead in the 12th minute through October's player of the month, Ade Adeyinka. David Przybylo played him through the middle and, from around 20 yards out, he unleashed an unstoppable strike into the net to give Bridges an early advantage.

The remainder of the first half was evenly matched, with Roberts producing several good saves in the Bridges goal. At the other end, the energetic Villavicencio continued to cause problems for the visitors, particularly with a dangerous cross towards Reece Hallard, whose header from six yards narrowly missed the target.

It could have been 2-0 moments into the second half as, straight from the restart, Bridges put together an excellent move down the right. Velvick released Przybylo through on goal, but under pressure from the goalkeeper, his effort narrowly drifted wide.

Brentwood began to pile on the pressure at the other end and found their reward in the 58th minute. Following a free-kick from Reid, Gibbs slotted his effort into the bottom corner to restore parity.

Just a couple of minutes later, Przybylo, who had been a constant handful for the visitors, worked his way past his marker and had another opportunity to restore Bridges' advantage, but his effort narrowly missed the far post.

With Bridges short of options on the bench and several players playing through injuries, maintaining the intensity became increasingly difficult. Brentwood Town took control during the final 20 minutes, but Roberts came to the rescue with two superb saves to deny Cerulli and Sears, while the Bridges players continued to put their bodies on the line to protect their point.

There was late drama in the 94th minute when Reece Hallard chased a ball through on the counter-attack, only for Cripps to handle it as the last man.

The Brentwood defender was shown a straight red card, leaving the visitors down to ten men. However, it came too late to have any impact on the outcome, as the match finished 1-1.

The point represents a much-needed response from Bridges, who are now into a demanding run of three consecutive away fixtures in seven days against Cray Wanderers (Tuesday 10), Welling United (Saturday 14) and Dartford (Tuesday 17).

Three Bridges: Roberts, Irving, Neathey (C), Malone, J. Hallard, Villavicencio (Chambers 87), Adeyinka, Przybylo (Richards 77), Velvick, R. Hallard, Bennett.

Player of the Match – Sam Roberts: An impressive display from Roberts in goal, producing several outstanding saves to keep Bridges in the contest. The young goalkeeper also impressed with his distribution, setting his side on the attack with some excellent kicking. A thoroughly deserved Player of the Match award.

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase has received final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to charter Coinbase National Trust Company, the crypto exchange said on Monday.

The OCC approved the application last Friday, according to Coinbase. The firm said the charter will let it expand its custody business, which holds digital assets on behalf of institutional clients, under a federal regulatory framework.

"Custody is foundational to our business," the company said, describing itself as the leading crypto custodian globally. The charter will "bring clear and consistent federal uniformity" to its custody operations, it added.

"Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong tweeted on Monday, calling the trust company "another step for building that foundation."

Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain.

Coinbase National Trust Company is another step for building that foundation. https://t.co/35K2yC3xpj

— Brian Armstrong (@brian_armstrong) November 9, 2026

Coinbase has offered regulated custody since 2018, when its Coinbase Custody Trust Company won a limited purpose trust charter from the New York Department of Financial Services. The new national trust company builds on that foundation while bringing its trust activities into a consistent federal framework, Coinbase said.

The approval comes seven months after the OCC gave Coinbase conditional approval in April, when the company said it was not becoming a commercial bank and would not take retail deposits or engage in fractional reserve banking. Coinbase first applied for the charter last year.

Coinbase joins a growing list of crypto firms with national trust charters. Circle won final approval in July for a trust bank to manage the reserves behind its USDC stablecoin, and Ripple received final approval last month for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin.

Others are still awaiting final approval. Jack Dorsey's Block has applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin in September, Kraken parent Payward filed its own application in May, and a trust company linked to Trump family-backed World Liberty Financial won conditional approval in August for a charter tied to its USD1 stablecoin, Reuters reported.

The approvals have drawn opposition. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's crypto charter approvals, including Coinbase's, are illegal, and the Independent Community Bankers of America has sued the OCC in an effort to stop crypto firms from entering banking through what it describes as a side door.

philippe jarrigeon finds the extraordinary in nature, from twisted trees to surreal encounters

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

drawing on fashion imagery, pop culture, and surrealist staging, the french photographer creates playful scenes that challenge familiar ways of looking at the natural world.

French photographer Philippe Jarrigeon looks to the contorted beech trees of Champagne for Le Vrai du Faux, his series presented at Paris Photo 2026, where he is also the recipient of the eighth Prix Maison Ruinart.

The project follows Rocher Fatal, his second book published by RVB Books, which brings together two years of photographs made in the Forest of Fontainebleau. Moving from the woodland landscapes of Fontainebleau to the Champagne region, Jarrigeon finds inspiration in unusual natural forms, including the contorted beech trees known as the Faux de Verzy. Their twisting branches seem almost impossible to have occurred naturally, making them fitting subjects for his playful approach to photography.

Jarrigeon takes this strangeness as a starting point, bringing the trees into conversation with shells, flowers, animals, and objects. His photographs play with the idea of what nature can be, and with the curious possibility that the world might contain forms of life and ways of being that sit outside our usual understanding.

Jarrigeon’s approach to photography has developed over nearly two decades, moving between editorial commissions, commercial work, and personal projects. After graduating from ECAL (École cantonale d’art de Lausanne) in 2006, he began building a practice shaped by the visual languages of fashion, design, advertising, and popular culture.

His photographs often borrow from the polished aesthetics of fashion campaigns and the saturated imagery of 1980s and 1990s popular culture. Vivid colors and elaborate compositions give his images an immediately recognizable visual character. Yet Jarrigeon rarely uses these references straightforwardly. Humor, irony, and unexpected details introduce a sense of play and disrupt the apparent perfection of the scenes.

In Rocher Fatal, Philippe Jarrigeon spends two years photographing the Forest of Fontainebleau, approaching it as a setting for a series of curious episodes. Published by RVB Books with a text by Laura Vazquez, his second book brings together abandoned castles, dormant quarries, hikers, deer, and a fallen knight. A smiling flower and a seemingly ordinary rock also find their way into the story, alongside appearances by artists Nina Childress, Théo Mercier, Vimala Pons, Laura Vazquez, and Rebeka Warrior.

The photographs have a cinematic quality, but Jarrigeon’s sense of humor keeps the scenes from becoming too serious. The forest is at once recognizable and strangely theatrical, with its trees and inhabitants becoming part of the same narrative. In the years following the photographs, fires damaged parts of the Fontainebleau forest, affecting its trees, habitats, and well-used paths. The book also recalls a landscape that has since undergone significant damage.

In Le Vrai du Faux, Jarrigeon continues this exploration in the Champagne region, where geological history and living forms become material for an imaginative photographic project. The Faux de Verzy, with their unusually twisted trunks and branches, are among its central subjects. Their shapes give the trees a supernatural presence, and Jarrigeon photographs them as though they were characters.

Other images introduce shells, remnants of a sea that once covered the region, alongside bouquets, luminous forms, and ambiguous figures. In The Harvest, Poppy, and The Surprise, familiar natural elements take on an unfamiliar character through composition and staging. The presence of animals and human figures complicates the distinction between observer and subject, while objects appear to belong as naturally to the landscape as the plants and creatures around them.

Jarrigeon describes the project as an invitation to look beyond nature as scenery. ‘With Le Vrai du Faux, nature is never treated as a mere backdrop; it becomes a source of inspiration and a space for imagination. My experience of Champagne has shaped a vision of landscapes inhabited by presences, spirits, remnants of the past, and fictions,’ the photographer notes.

Jarrigeon avoids the usual solemn approach to nature, creating images in which trees, flowers, shells, animals, and man-made objects appear side by side. The combinations are often unexpected, giving familiar elements a different character.

The photographs also move away from a human-centered view of the natural world. People are only one presence among many, sharing the scenes with plants, animals, and objects. Jarrigeon leaves the relationships between them open, allowing the images to move between the recognizable and the absurd.

Le Vrai du Faux is presented by WOMANRAY in the Émergence section of Paris Photo, taking place at the Grand Palais from November 12 to 15, 2026. The project also marks Jarrigeon’s selection for the Prix Maison Ruinart. Meanwhile, his solo exhibition Rocher Fatal is on view at WOMANRAY in Paris through December 24, bringing together work from his recent exploration of the Forest of Fontainebleau.

upcoming presentation: Le Vrai du Faux – Paris Photo 2026, Émergence section venue: Grand Palais, Paris, France dates: November 12–15, 2026

Geyoro inspires London City Lionesses to 4-1 WSL win over Birmingham City

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

London City Lionesses ended a four-match winless run against Birmingham City on Sunday 8 November, winning 4-1 in the Women’s Super League. Eder Maestre’s side led 2-0 at half-time in Dartford and finished with 20 shots to Birmingham’s 4. Their last win over Birmingham came on 12 February 2023.

Poppy Pattinson opened the scoring after 10 minutes. Six minutes later Delphine Cascarino doubled the lead from a Grace Geyoro assist. Cascarino was booked for a foul in the 27th minute, and María Pérez followed her into the referee’s notebook in the 37th.

Amy Merricks’s side halved the deficit five minutes after the restart. Océane Hurtré scored in the 50th minute, set up by Lisa Naalsund. It was Birmingham’s only shot on target all afternoon, and Hurtré’s display earned her a rating of 8.2.

Neve Grace Herron was cautioned for dissent in the 72nd minute. Two minutes later Geyoro scored herself, restoring the two-goal margin. With a goal and an assist, she finished rated 8.82, the highest on the pitch.

Daniela Arques was booked in the 81st minute, the hosts’ third yellow card. The last word went to Kadidiatou Diani, who came off the bench for 45 minutes and scored in the second minute of stoppage time from a Freya Godfrey assist. That was Diani’s fourth league goal of the season, eighth in the WSL scoring chart.

The home side dominated the numbers. They had 67% of the ball, completed 604 of 683 passes and put 8 efforts on target, forcing 4 saves from Baggaley. Earps did not have a save to make. Expected goals finished 1.52 to 0.19.

The win leaves London City Lionesses third with 19 points from 9 games, 4 behind Chelsea and 6 behind leaders Manchester City. It was their fourth win in six matches in all competitions, coming straight after a 2-1 defeat by Manchester City.

Birmingham are 13th on 6 points, 6 clear of Charlton Athletic. This was a second straight 4-1 defeat after the loss to Tottenham Hotspur. Liverpool, in 12th, are 2 points ahead.

Birmingham have a quick chance to respond. They host Sunderland in the WSL Cup on Wednesday 11 November, while London City Lionesses travel to Arsenal in the league on Saturday 14 November.

Speedrunners Didn't Take Long To Find New Ways To Break Ocarina Of Time

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Ocarina of Time has only been out on Switch 2 for a matter of days, but that has proven more than enough time for the speedrunning community to find a bunch of new exploits in it (thanks for the heads-up, IGN).

Within the first few hours, those who know the N64 game inside and out were hard at work finding ways to cut corners or otherwise break the game to wrap things up as quickly as possible.

Zelda speedrunner gymnast89 managed to hit credits in as little as four and a half hours on the day after the game's launch (we were still marvelling at Kokiri Forest's water effects at that point), and the skip techniques have only multiplied since then.

The biggie at the time of writing is a new glitch that sends Link soaring into the sky, bypassing a bunch of puzzles and looking particularly badass as he does so. Speedrunner ZFG was seemingly the first to discover the move, which relies on some careful positioning around the game's Magic Bean flowers. A series of hops and skips with the camera angle just right, and the game's physics system goes into panic mode, giving Link a real impressive vertical jump.

It doesn't seem to be tied to Shadow Temple skips, either. Other players have found that you can replicate a similar move from the comfort of an empty chest, flitting between Ocarina tracks or using specific items to once again send Link on the up and up.

But what about those sections of the speedrun where Link needs to be on Terra Firma? The remake boasts a brand new dash button for quicker on-foot travel, but there must be an even quicker way, right?

Well, it looks like it! u/Pomps8a took to Reddit with the following strat, which lets Link roll quicker by interrupting the animation with an attack at the end. It's perhaps not the most elegant solution, and there doesn't seem to be any one-to-one matchup at the time of writing to see how much quicker it is, but anything that helps shave off valuable milliseconds is always handy to those in the know.

There's another glitch doing the rounds that lets speedrunners hop over previously inaccessible ledges by item swapping in mid-air. So, if the above big jump is too much, then at least they've got the option of a smaller big jump.

And for those wondering, no, all of this manipulation doesn't work wonders for Link's physical well-being. Skipping big chunks of the game means that a bunch of the remake's new cutscenes are thrown off whack — with Child Link appearing in Adult Link clips, and vice versa — and the Hero of Time looks all the worse for it.

Just look at the poor boy in the following post-sequence break screens from ZFG's recent speedrunning stream:

Honestly, we've been laughing at the above 'Lonk' and 'Lunk' for longer than we care to admit. Look at his little face!

We knew it wouldn't take long for the speedrunning community to find ways to break the remake, but we'll admit that this many glitches so early is rather impressive. We're sure that even more will be unearthed in the coming days/months/years.

Even without all of the glitches, Ocarina of Time is still a damn good time on Switch 2. We awarded the game a 9/10 in our review, calling it "unquestionably the best way to play one of the most seminal games of all time".

West Ham face Taty Castellanos, River Plate transfer exit threat

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

As West Ham continue to fall short of rekindling their fortunes despite Scott Parker's recent appointment, the possibility of Taty Castellanos departing the London Stadium in the coming months has now emerged in a development that will spark mixed reactions from Hammers supporters.

West Ham's return to the Championship following 14 successive seasons of solid Premier League standing is not going to plan right now, with the East Londoners appearing increasingly unlikely to seal a previously-expected top-flight return at the very first time of asking as champions.

A mixed run of form featuring consecutive home defeats at home to a pair of promotion rivals in Southampton and West Bromwich Albion ultimately ended Nuno Espirito Santo's unpopular tenure in charge, and the Portuguese boss has been swiftly succeeded by former Hammers midfielder Parker.

The ex-Bournemouth and Fulham boss, having already achieved three promotions from the second-tier with as many clubs after most recently guiding Burnley back to the promised land in 2025 before his dismissal towards the end of the previous campaign, has encountered a challenging return to the club thus far, though, taking just one point from his opening two matches.

Last Wednesday's thrilling 3-3 comeback draw at Cardiff City after overturning a three-goal defecit at half-time was followed up by a disappointing 2-1 defeat away to Blackburn Rovers over the weekend, and with the Hammers yet to enjoy the anticipated upturn under Parker, they now face the threat of losing one of their big-name performers once the January window rolls around.

Castellanos has endured a particularly difficult and underwhelming introduction to the Championship, which the Argentine forward was expected to light up after his mid-season arrival back in January preceded encouraging goalscoring form in spite of his side's ultimate failure to dodge the drop.

Six goals from 18 Premier League appearances means that the current return of just one strike across 14 second-tier fixtures - nine of which have been starts - is not what fans were anticipating, and they had presented vocal criticism towards the divisive 28-year-old for a number of weeks before Parker hauled the attacker off at the interval against the Bluebirds and then dropped his name out of the starting line-up at Ewood Park.

It has now emerged that Castellanos could leave East London as early as January, with a fresh report from Argentine-based outlet El Intransigente claiming strong long-term interest from River Plate.

As per the update, River Plate have held interest in the two-cap Argentine international's services in recent windows and he represents a "top target" in the eyes of manager Leonardo Ponzio.

Blackburn Rovers captain Todd Cantwell has been among those reacting to the club's dig towards Scott Parker's West Ham after Saturday's 2-1 victory

It's said that the Buenos Aries-based giants feel strongly about Castellano's profile and international experience, with the striker having risen to prominence outside of his native Argentina in Uruguay with Montevideo City Torque before enjoying productive spells at New York City, Girona and Lazio ahead of joining West Ham earlier this year for a reported £26 million figure on a four-and-a-half year contract.

West Ham supporters have grown increasingly critical of Castellanos and his long-term future under Parker remains unclear at this moment in time, although a fresh hurdle could prevent River Plate from sealing the deal.

River Plate have been presented with a significant obstacle to overcome if they intend on returning Castellanos to his homeland, as it's a move which wouldn't come cheaply for the 38-time Primera Division champions.

Indeed, the report has added that the salary Castellanos earns at the London Stadium is the main challenge for River Plate right now, leaving the club to weigh up alternative options such as acquiring the sought-after frontman on loan or even purchasing a percentage of his rights.

Both of those options, of course, would require the prior approval of West Ham, whose own stance on potentially parting ways with the ex-Lazio striker just a year after his arrival with a club likely unable to recoup the figure of purchase is also not yet known.

It's noted that Castellanos' lengthy contract with the Championship promotion hopefuls puts Parker's side in a comfortable negotiating decision, and hold all the cards as River Plate will not be able to wrap up any deal without their support.

Interestingly, however, the update also makes reference to previous interest elsewhere, reminding that Castellanos had reportedly wished to leave West Ham in the summer amid loan interest from Seville-based rivals Real Betis and Sevilla that never materialised.

The London Stadium faithful remain very much divided on Castellanos at the moment, with some making their minds up on the out-of-form forward and others willing to persist with further patience.

Prior claims of a January exit if the price is met at the end of the previous month saw some West Ham fans refuse to hold back on their feelings about Castellanos' future, and this one fan believes that his hapless form in the Championship would make for no great loss if he was to depart.

Another would be happy to see the Hammers striker leave at any cost, even if the club themselves surely won't take that stance.

Castellanos has left some frustrated for reasons beyond his inability to find the back of the net on a regular basis, too, and having formerly scored at a relatively-prolific rate leading the line for a side relegated from the Premier League, his work-rate and application have been called into question.

His display in the aforementioned draw at Cardiff drew particular criticism, and West Ham were a much more fluid, functional and threatening outfit once the Argentine was withdrawn for Joel Piroe at the break.

Now, with the Suriname international scoring in successive encounters, it's unclear how Castellanos will force his way back into starting contention ahead of a positional rival with a historically-goal-laden hit-rate at this level.

West Ham fans, how do you feel about Taty Castellanos' future? Would you hold onto the striker, or is it time to cash in? What should his valuation be? Let us know your thoughts in the comments below!

But there are other fans who continue to stand by Castellanos, and considering how he fared at the highest level last time out, it's no great surprise that certain supporters still believe he can come good in the second-tier.

Either way, there are plenty of West Ham fans who, despite the criticism of the majority, do feel that the divisive striker would thrive in a system that emphasises chance creation under a manager willing to trust in his services and get the best out of an undeniably-talented striker with pedigree in more prestigious and higher-quality divisions.

This punter, meanwhile, reckons that cashing in on Castellanos would be a "stupid decision", but only time will tell to see what action West Ham decide to take and whether a deal can successfully be thrashed out with River Plate.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Thoughts of a lifelong fan: Where to start?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Where to start? Yesterday was not good enough. It wasn’t good enough from the players, the coach and yes, from some of our supporters too. When we arrived at Wimborne Stadium, after the half mile walk from the car park, spirits were high with some fans predicting an easy victory, whereas I would have taken a win on penalties if it had been on offer there and then. We all know we didn’t get that; we couldn’t blame the referee as none of the ref’s decisions would have affected the result, and we have to accept that on the day Wimborne wanted it more than we appeared to.

I hope you have noticed the use of “we” in my writing, because that’s what following and supporting any team in any sport should be about. I was at Old Trafford when we went a goal down and the level of support we gave increased, I was also at Wrexham when we lost four-one in the promotion season and were complimented by the Welsh fans on how we stuck with our team throughout the whole game.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

The difference yesterday? Apart from some players giving their all there appeared to be no plan of action to get the right result. I can’t accept it was all down to the players though, as the coach would seem to suggest. I believe that we have enough quality players in our bottom of the table side, not to be where we currently sit at the nadir of the 92 and out of all the cups that really should matter to us. I believe that supporting a team must be shown more when things aren’t going right, and if that means cheering your team on even when they are losing to a poor side, who would normally be beaten easily, then so be it.

Nobody covered themselves in glory yesterday, the coach, players and us the fans.  Some of the “us” were too quiet whilst some chose to be negatively noisy, demolishing, rather than supporting the players on the pitch.

You can tell I’m not a happy devil, but will I be there at the next game to cheer the players on? Yes, I will, and I’ll keep on supporting them no matter what.

PS Why wasn’t Ade Adeyemo brought on Yesterday Kevin, when I believe he could have made the difference.

Heysel ’85 is the definitive, eye-opening drama that finally looks past Liverpool terrace narrative

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Play the viral 442GOALS game and build an XI of Premier League legends!
