Sign up to our FREE live football newsletter, tracking all of the biggest games available to watch on the device of your choice. Never miss a kick-off!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

FourFourTwo attended the 2026 London Film Festival for the screening of 'Heysel 85', which will be released in cinemas this November

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For the past 40 years, the tragedy of the 1985 European Cup final has often been reduced in mainstream British discourse to a single, monolithic headline: football hooliganism.

In Heysel ’85, director Teodora Mihai bypasses the familiar cliches of terrace culture to produce something which seeks to change the conversation surrounding the disaster. Coming from a documentary background, Mihai approaches the disaster not as a reflection of senseless violence, but as a study into institutional breakdown and the callous, self-preserving instincts of political power.

The film anchors its narrative through two parallel viewpoints. The principal perspective belongs to Marie Dumont (Violet Braeckman), the fictional press attaché and daughter to the Mayor of Brussels.

Braeckman, a polyglot who learned Italian specifically for the production, delivers a performance of quiet, escalating dread as she watches the veneer of civic competence crumble from within.

Operating on the other side of the crisis is Luca Rossi (Matteo Simoni), an Italian broadcast journalist reporting live from the ground while desperately trying to locate his father, uncle, and brother, all attending the match as Juventus supporters.

Early on, Mihai builds tension through procedural unease. Disorienting, fluid tracking shots wind through subterranean corridors, establishing a sense of foreboding long before the tragic events unfold. Shot in an empty hospital in Antwerp, the oppressive concrete hallways reflect the popular view that the Heysel Stadium, built in 1930, was run-down and unfit for a fixture of such magnitude by the mid-1980s, something which is referenced in the film.

The film avoids the trap of laying blame at the feet of ticket touts, generic mobs, or rowdy Liverpool supporters. It is deeply sympathetic to the ordinary fans caught up in the events of May 29, 1985, casting a forensic eye upward at the organisers.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

UEFA executives, the local municipal government, and a fractured security apparatus are all painted in an unflattering light. The territorial friction between the municipal police and the Belgian Gendarmerie is laid bare; their reluctance and inability to share intelligence or coordinate chain-of-command turns an already combustible situation into an avoidable tragedy.

Nowhere is this administrative failure more grotesque than in the depiction of the Mayor of Brussels (played by Josse De Pauw). Portrayed as a sozzled, cowardly executive, his characterisation is rooted in historical reality. According to Braeckman, De Pauw knew the real-life mayor Hervé Brouhon, a figure regarding whom it was openly joked in local political circles that any serious decisions had to be made before 11am.

The film captures the hollow facade of authority dissipating in direct proportion to his alcohol intake. In one particularly telling sequence early on, Marie is dispatched to fetch the key to the stadium’s liquor cabinet so the mayor and his dignitaries can enjoy a proper drink. It is a bitterly ironic snapshot of municipal priorities just hours before 39 people would lose their lives.

When catastrophe strikes, the narrative strips away any remaining shred of civil propriety. Mihai’s documentary sensibilities ensure the integration of genuine, unaltered archive footage feels seamless rather than exploitative, anchoring the fictional protagonists in historical reality. While the Italian delegation emerges with a degree of grace and anguish, UEFA and local authorities retreat into corporate damage control, consumed entirely by liability, protocol, and the insistence that the match must still be played.

By refusing to take the easy narrative route of sensationalising terrace thuggery, Heysel ’85 delivers an important, unsettling reassessment of the catastrophe. It is an indictment of systemic cowardice that proves human lives were not lost merely to crowd surges, but to the arrogance of an establishment that abdicated responsibility long before kick-off - and, tellingly, never resigned in its wake.

Following its UK premiere at the 70th BFI London Film Festival, Heysel ’85 will be released in UK cinemas on 20 November 2026, distributed by Icon Film Distribution.

Joe joined FourFourTwo as senior digital writer in July 2025 after five years covering Leeds United in the Championship and Premier League. Joe's 'Mastermind' specialist subject is 2000s-era Newcastle United having had a season ticket at St. James' Park for 10 years before relocating to Leeds and later London. Joe takes a keen interest in youth football, covering PL2, U21 Euros, as well as U20 and U17 World Cups in the past, in addition to hosting the industry-leading football recruitment-focused SCOUTED podcast. He is also one of the lucky few to have 'hit top bins' as a contestant on Soccer AM. It wasn't a shin-roller.

We Keep Going Back To Germany. This Family Trip Reminded Me Why.

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

We landed in Zürich on SAS and drove to my in-law’s home in Southwest Germany, about an hour away.

There are border checks now as you enter Germany from France and Switzerland, though usually (I’m just saying…) when they see it’s white people they just waive you through.

And I’ll leave off the politics here (because I’d rather focus on getting our own house in order in the USA), but I think this picture says it all about Germany these days:

We tend to stop at DM (a drug store chain) at the start of each trip because it’s on the way home and we always come with a list since so many products here are cheaper and better than their American counterparts, even with the stronger Euro. One thing I like about DM (well, liked…) was that they had a free water dispenser with cups.

In Germany water is an annoyance when going out…most restaurants and coffee shops frown on tap water (unlike in France and the Netherlands), so you’re paying everywhere €5-8EUR for bottles you can buy for 50-80 Euro cents in the grocery store. It’s nice to “hydrate” at DM, but for TWO YEARS the water machine has been broken.

How in the heck can you not fix this for two years (unless, as I suppose, the point is that you don’t want to offer free water any longer)?!

It was a bit warm for the time of the year, but not unpleasant. How nice it was to be back and over the next several days we enjoyed a lot of quality time together, with good döner, coffee, and ice cream.

There was a fairly intense lighting storm that was just beautiful to sit out on the patio and watch:

One morning I decided to ride by myself to Basel for coffee…there was a new place I wanted to try called Mantila Coffee…it was SO GOOD (my new favorite coffee spot in Basel) and I had such a nice chat with the owner. I’m going to update my Basel Coffee post with a couple of places I tried on this trip.

Augustine was livid that biked to Basel without him, so we returned the next day (sadly Mantila is closed on Saturday), but we found another great place called Tinka. Augustine wanted his own coffee…I had a cortado, he had cappuccino, and then we split a delicious sparkling blood orange drink.

My father-in-law likes a restaurant called Gasthaus Krone in the hamlet of Lehnacker, located really right in the middle of the Schwarzwald (Black Forest). The food is delicious indeed.

One cool part of this trip is that my daughter finally got the hang of riding her bike herself…we just don’t feel it’s safe to ride bikes at home (due to fast drivers…see here for a laugh) but it was fun to see her so excited.

To celebrate, we had (more) ice cream together.

Finally, to be clear, the trip did not happen this weekend. I delay these reports for my own privacy and the protection of my family…as I’ve mentioned before, sadly there are monsters out there (I’ll just leave it at that…). But what a lovely and unexpected getaway this was and I am so grateful for all the time spent in Germany.

Time in the city and time in the country. Travel and family. Time spent in community (faith based or otherwise). Coffee. All are non-optional for a well lived life. Hope you enjoyed the trip.

U15 : de la joie contre les Caladois

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Les U15 de Benjamin Petit pouvaient être heureux à la fin de leur match contre les Caladois du FC Villefranche Beaujolais. A l'issue d'une rencontre très disputée, comme souvent dans cet exigeant championnat, ils n'ont rien lâché face à un adversaire de valeur et ont su trouver les ressources pour l'emporter. Cela récompense leur volonté d'aller de l'avant, leur ténacité et leur concentration dans l'aspect défensif. Sans quelques joueurs retenus avec les U16 de Kevin De Jesus (Ilan Clapeyron, Riad Moutae, Kaïs Boukerma et Hugo Durin) tous ont su se mettre au diapason, et cette victoire est celle d'un collectif très solidaire.

L'entame du match a été stéphanoise, les Verts s'installant dans le camp caladois. Dès la 9ème minute, sur un centre venu de la gauche, Nolan Lebreton remettait pour Charly Davignon qui était devancé de peu par un défenseur. Deux minutes plus tard, un coup-franc de ce même Nolan Lebreton était capté par le gardien. Les Verts monopolisaient le ballon mais le dernier geste, souvent dans les petits espaces, manquait de justesse pour mettre de conclure. Peu à peu, Villefranche déserrait l'étreinte stéphanoise et se montrait dangereux sur coup de pied arrêté, mais Manoa Frison faisait bonne garde, notamment, à la 26ème, où il réalisait une double parade avant que Victor Hamouma ne s'interpose pour stopper la tentative adverse. Quelques minutes plus tard, Charly Davignon, qui repiquait au centre, était victime d'une faute laquelle donnait lieu à un coup-franc. Nolhan Fernandes le frappait et mettait le gardien à contribution. Peu après sur un long centre de Nolhan Fernandes, Hugo Bastide obtenait un corner. Ce corner, joué à deux, permettait à Nolan Lebreton d'adresser un long centre au deuxième poteau que le portier caladois dégageait. Les contres de Villefranche butaient sur une solide défense stéphanoise, concentrée et rigoureuse qui gérait bien les espaces et s'attachait à ressortir proprement les ballons. Le score était donc nul et vierge au repos.

La deuxième mi-temps repartait sur les mêmes bases, avec une maitrîse verte retrouvée et des situations intéressantes, sans parvenir toutefois à faire la différence.  Mais les Verts, suite à un carton blanc à la 54ème minute, se retrouvaient réduits à 10 pendant une dizaine de minutes, et ce fait de jeu freinait un peu leur dynamique. Ils allaient trouver la faille suite à un cafouillage dans la surface, avec une main adverse et un accrochage sur Mathis La Rosa. Toujours est-il que l'arbitre sanctionnait cette action d'un penalty que Charly Davignon transformait, donnant l'avantage aux Verts (68ème). La fin du match allait être plutôt hâchée avec plusieurs coups-de-pied arrêtés que Villefranche jouait directement. Les Verts se créaient cependant quelques opportunités mais un manque de justesse ne leur permettait pas de les concrétiser. Le match se terminait donc sur cette victoire stéphanoise somme toute logique, tant elle avait été bien construite.

La composition : Frison - Bergougnoux (Uneau, 50ème), L. La Rosa, Hamouma, Bastide - Wojcik (Davignon, 50ème), M.La Rosa, Lebreton - Fernandes (Madjidi, 50ème), Maganga Verrier, Davignon (Linganzi, 41ème).

La réaction de Benjamin Petit : "Le match a été serré, très disputé face à une bonne équipe de Villefranche, comme souvent dans ce championnat. Il n'y a pas de match facile et c'est un bon apprentissage pour les jeunes. Nous avons fait vingt premières minutes plutôt intéressantes, avec de la maîtrise, une volonté de défendre en avançant, un point que travaillons. Nous avons baissé en fin de première mi-temps, avec plus de difficulté à maintenir notre pression, ce qui nous a menés à défendre plus bas. En conclusion, je dirais que globalement, nous avons eu la maîtrise, mais mon regret est le manque de justesse, de percussion et d'efficacité pour créer du danger sur le but adverse et se mettre à l'abri. Je retiens notre solidité défensive, notre rigueur dans les prises en charge, notre détermination à ne rien encaisser, notamment sur les coups-de-pied arrêtés, un domaine dans lequel nous avons progressé. Il faudra néanmoins éviter certaines fautes, évitables, qui offrent des situations dangereuses à l'adversaire. C'était un match intéressant dans l'apprentissage, la gestion des temps forts et des temps faibles. Il faut toutefois  essayer d'être plus efficace dans nos temps forts tout en restant solides dans les temps faibles."
